People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose what finally brought me in is that I just... I don't feel like myself anymore. I used to make time for so many things, like painting, hiking, singing in the choir, and our big Sunday family gatherings. Which, actually, my sister has been hosting lately because she finally finished her kitchen remodel. It took them almost eight months, can you believe that? The contractors just kept delaying and delaying because of some supply chain issue with the cabinets, or maybe it was the tile. She did get this gorgeous farmhouse sink out of it, though. I told her she should put some little potted succulents by the window above it, since it gets that good morning light... 

But, um, anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Just that... now, even when I have a free hour to myself, I don't even want to go over there. I just sit there. I'm completely unable to care about any of those things I used to love. I just sit in a chair and can't bring myself to care.

Yes, exactly, like I was just saying... I just really don't care about any of it anymore. Even the choir. I used to never miss a Wednesday rehearsal. I do. It's like... most days I feel like there's this heavy gray film over absolutely everything. I can be standing right there in a classroom full of students—I'm a teaching assistant, mostly with fourth graders this year. And they are usually just so full of energy. Just yesterday, one of the boys, Toby, brought in this massive caterpillar he found by the playground fence. It was bright green with these little yellow spots. The lead teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually wants to get a terrarium so the kids can watch it turn into a butterfly. Which I think is a lovely idea, really, though I don't know who is going to take care of it over the weekends. I guess she will take it home, or maybe the janitor... 

But, um... sorry. What I mean is, I can be standing right in the middle of all that life in the classroom, or even just sitting at home on the couch with my husband, and I still just feel completely hollow inside. I'm just always right on the edge of tears, no matter what's going on around me.

My sleep has been a complete mess, honestly. It's been all over the place for months now. I'll just lie there in bed at night, staring at the ceiling, and my brain just won't shut off. I keep replaying all these details from this grant application I was helping the school with... which reminds me, the principal, Mr. Henderson, he's been talking about using the grant money to renovate the old computer lab. They want to put in these new interactive smartboards, which I guess are supposed to be better for the kids' eyes than the old projectors. I remember when we just had chalkboards, and you had to clap the erasers outside. The dust would get everywhere, all over your hands and clothes... 

Oh, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yes, my sleep. I just lie awake thinking about the grant, and then I finally drift off, but I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. And then I'm just up. Even on the rare nights when I do manage to sleep a little longer, I still get up feeling like I never rested at all. I'm just so tired.

I don't really think so. I haven't noticed moving any slower or anything. I mean, I sit at my little table in Mrs. Gable's room quite a bit. Though, she just ordered these new chairs for the kids—those wobbly stools, you know? They're supposed to help the kids who can't sit still. Toby has one, and he's always bouncing on it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do feel guilty a lot. It's like... well, for example, whenever anything goes wrong with the curriculum project I'm helping with at school, even something really small, I just spiral. I keep thinking I'm completely letting the students down. And then I start feeling like I'm just wasting all the sacrifices my parents made for me to have this kind of opportunity in the first place, you know, to get my degree and work in education. 

They worked so incredibly hard. My dad was at the textile mill for thirty years. He used to bring home these wooden spools of thread that were almost empty, and my mom would use them for her sewing. She actually made all my clothes when I was little. I had this one dress, it was yellow with little white daisies on it, and I wore it for my fifth-grade picture. Remember how they used to do those school pictures with the weird laser backgrounds? Or the fake library bookshelf backgrounds. I always chose the bookshelf because I loved reading so much. I used to go to the public library every Saturday and check out the maximum number of books allowed. The librarian, Mrs. Gable—no relation to the teacher I work with now, just a coincidence—she would always save the new mystery novels for me... 

But anyway, yes, the guilt. It just feels like I'm failing the kids and failing my parents if the curriculum project isn't absolutely perfect.

They're supposed to help the kids who can't sit still. Toby has one, and he's always bouncing on it. It makes this squeaking noise on the linoleum that drives me a little crazy. The janitor, Mr. Davis, said he could put tennis balls on the bottom of them to help with the noise. People used to do that with walkers, remember? My grandmother had a walker with bright yellow tennis balls on the back legs. She used to walk down to the corner store every single morning to buy a newspaper, back when people still read the physical paper. I... well, I don't really want to actively hurt myself. But sometimes, when I'm up late at night in my little studio room at home—that's where I do all the cutting and laminating for Mrs. Gable's class—I do have these thoughts. We actually turned the spare bedroom into that studio space after my son went off to college. He's at State now, studying civil engineering. I still can't believe how fast the time went. I was just looking at his old science fair projects the other day. He made this papier-mâché volcano in the third grade, and we used way too much baking soda and red food coloring. It erupted everywhere and completely ruined the kitchen rug. It was this hideous green shag rug anyway, so my husband, Mark, wasn't too upset about it. He actually spent that entire weekend tearing it up and putting down that snap-together laminate flooring. It looks like real wood, but it's so much easier to clean, especially when you're tracking in dirt from the garden... 

Oh, gosh, I'm sorry. The thoughts. Right. It's just... on those late nights in the studio, I sometimes catch myself thinking that it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. I haven't made a plan or anything, I promise. But just having those thoughts pop into my head... they really scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told Mark about it the other day, because I realized I just couldn't keep handling them alone.

Just yesterday, one of the boys, Toby, brought in this massive caterpillar he found by the playground fence. It was bright green with these little yellow spots. The lead teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually wants to get a terrarium so the kids can watch it turn into a butterfly. Which I think is a lovely idea, really, though I don't know who is going to take care of it over the weekends. I guess she will take it home, or maybe the janitor... \n\nBut, um... sorry. What I mean is, I can be standing right in the middle of all that life in the classroom, or even just sitting at home on the couch with my husband, and I still just feel completely hollow inside. I'm just always right on the edge of tears, no matter what's going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think my weight has changed much. My clothes still fit the same. Though, speaking of clothes, I was trying to find my favorite blue cardigan the other day—the one with the pearl buttons. I bought it on clearance at that little boutique downtown, the one that used to be a bakery? They still have the original tin ceiling in there, it's really beautiful. The owner said she was thinking of painting it copper, but I told her she should leave it silver. It really opens up the space. \n\nBut anyway... um, no. I haven't noticed any weight changes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been a complete mess, honestly. It's been all over the place for months now. I'll just lie there in bed at night, staring at the ceiling, and my brain just won't shut off. I keep replaying all these details from this grant application I was helping the school with... which reminds me, the principal, Mr. Henderson, he's been talking about using the grant money to renovate the old computer lab. They want to put in these new interactive smartboards, which I guess are supposed to be better for the kids' eyes than the old projectors. I remember when we just had chalkboards, and you had to clap the erasers outside. The dust would get everywhere, all over your hands and clothes... \n\nOh, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yes, my sleep. I just lie awake thinking about the grant, and then I finally drift off, but I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. And then I'm just up. Even on the rare nights when I do manage to sleep a little longer, I still get up feeling like I never rested at all. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I haven't noticed moving any slower or anything. I mean, I sit at my little table in Mrs. Gable's room quite a bit. Though, she just ordered these new chairs for the kids—those wobbly stools, you know? They're supposed to help the kids who can't sit still. Toby has one, and he's always bouncing on it. It makes this squeaking noise on the linoleum that drives me a little crazy. The janitor, Mr. Davis, said he could put tennis balls on the bottom of them to help with the noise. People used to do that with walkers, remember? My grandmother had a walker with bright yellow tennis balls on the back legs. She used to walk down to the corner store every single morning to buy a newspaper, back when people still read the physical paper. Now everyone just looks at their phones for the news, which I think is why everyone is so stressed out all the time... \n\nBut, um, I'm sorry. No, I don't have any trouble sitting still myself. And I don't feel like I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. Just my normal pace, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do feel guilty a lot. It's like... well, for example, whenever anything goes wrong with the curriculum project I'm helping with at school, even something really small, I just spiral. I keep thinking I'm completely letting the students down. And then I start feeling like I'm just wasting all the sacrifices my parents made for me to have this kind of opportunity in the first place, you know, to get my degree and work in education. \n\nThey worked so incredibly hard. My dad was at the textile mill for thirty years. He used to bring home these wooden spools of thread that were almost empty, and my mom would use them for her sewing. She actually made all my clothes when I was little. I had this one dress, it was yellow with little white daisies on it, and I wore it for my fifth-grade picture. Remember how they used to do those school pictures with the weird laser backgrounds? Or the fake library bookshelf backgrounds. I always chose the bookshelf because I loved reading so much. I used to go to the public library every Saturday and check out the maximum number of books allowed. The librarian, Mrs. Gable—no relation to the teacher I work with now, just a coincidence—she would always save the new mystery novels for me... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the guilt. It just feels like I'm failing the kids and failing my parents if the curriculum project isn't absolutely perfect.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I... well, I don't really want to actively hurt myself. But sometimes, when I'm up late at night in my little studio room at home—that's where I do all the cutting and laminating for Mrs. Gable's class—I do have these thoughts. We actually turned the spare bedroom into that studio space after my son went off to college. He's at State now, studying civil engineering. I still can't believe how fast the time went. I was just looking at his old science fair projects the other day. He made this papier-mâché volcano in the third grade, and we used way too much baking soda and red food coloring. It erupted everywhere and completely ruined the kitchen rug. It was this hideous green shag rug anyway, so my husband, Mark, wasn't too upset about it. He actually spent that entire weekend tearing it up and putting down that snap-together laminate flooring. It looks like real wood, but it's so much easier to clean, especially when you're tracking in dirt from the garden... \n\nOh, gosh, I'm sorry. The thoughts. Right. It's just... on those late nights in the studio, I sometimes catch myself thinking that it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. I haven't made a plan or anything, I promise. But just having those thoughts pop into my head... they really scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told Mark about it the other day, because I realized I just couldn't keep handling them alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's been building for a while now... I'd say about eight months. It started right around the time I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. It sounded like such a wonderful idea at first, combining science and art. We were going to have the kids build these little solar-powered kinetic sculptures. I remember when I was a kid, we made sculptures out of popsicle sticks and Elmer's glue. We didn't have solar panels back then, of course. My brother used to eat the glue, actually. He's a dentist now, which is pretty funny when you think about it. He lives out in Colorado with his wife, Sarah. They just bought this beautiful A-frame cabin near the mountains, and they've been sending pictures of the snow. I haven't seen real snow in years, not since we took that ski trip when my son was in middle school. He broke his wrist on the bunny hill the very first day, and we spent the rest of the vacation playing Scrabble in the lodge... 

But, um, anyway. Yes, about eight months. Ever since that curriculum project started, things have just been getting heavier and heavier.

I used to make time for so many things, like painting, hiking, singing in the choir, and our big Sunday family gatherings. Which, actually, my sister has been hosting lately because she finally finished her kitchen remodel. It took them almost eight months, can you believe that? The contractors just kept delaying and delaying because of some supply chain issue with the cabinets, or maybe it was the tile. She did get this gorgeous farmhouse sink out of it, though. I told her she should put some little potted succulents by the window above it, since it gets that good morning light... \n\nBut, um, anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Just that... now, even when I have a free hour to myself, I don't even want to go over there. I just sit there. I'm completely unable to care about any of those things I used to love. I just sit in a chair and can't bring myself to care.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly, like I was just saying... I just really don't care about any of it anymore. Even the choir. I used to never miss a Wednesday rehearsal. We were actually supposed to be preparing for the spring concert soon, and usually, I love the spring concert because we do more upbeat pieces. Last year we did this beautiful medley, and my neighbor came to see it. She actually just got a new puppy, by the way, a little golden retriever mix, which is so cute but honestly it barks at all hours of the night... I think she's trying to crate train him, but it is just not going well. \n\nUm... I'm sorry, I'm getting off track again, aren't I? But yes. I've completely lost interest. Like I said, even when I have the time, I just sit in my chair now instead of doing any of the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I do. It's like... most days I feel like there's this heavy gray film over absolutely everything. I can be standing right there in a classroom full of students—I'm a teaching assistant, mostly with fourth graders this year. And they are usually just so full of energy. Just yesterday, one of the boys, Toby, brought in this massive caterpillar he found by the playground fence. It was bright green with these little yellow spots. The lead teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually wants to get a terrarium so the kids can watch it turn into a butterfly. Which I think is a lovely idea, really, though I don't know who is going to take care of it over the weekends. I guess she will take it home, or maybe the janitor... \n\nBut, um... sorry. What I mean is, I can be standing right in the middle of all that life in the classroom, or even just sitting at home on the couch with my husband, and I still just feel completely hollow inside. I'm just always right on the edge of tears, no matter what's going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think my weight has changed much. My clothes still fit the same. Though, speaking of clothes, I was trying to find my favorite blue cardigan the other day—the one with the pearl buttons. I bought it on clearance at that little boutique downtown, the one that used to be a bakery? They still have the original tin ceiling in there, it's really beautiful. The owner said she was thinking of painting it copper, but I told her she should leave it silver. It really opens up the space. \n\nBut anyway... um, no. I haven't noticed any weight changes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been a complete mess, honestly. It's been all over the place for months now. I'll just lie there in bed at night, staring at the ceiling, and my brain just won't shut off. I keep replaying all these details from this grant application I was helping the school with... which reminds me, the principal, Mr. Henderson, he's been talking about using the grant money to renovate the old computer lab. They want to put in these new interactive smartboards, which I guess are supposed to be better for the kids' eyes than the old projectors. I remember when we just had chalkboards, and you had to clap the erasers outside. The dust would get everywhere, all over your hands and clothes... \n\nOh, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yes, my sleep. I just lie awake thinking about the grant, and then I finally drift off, but I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. And then I'm just up. Even on the rare nights when I do manage to sleep a little longer, I still get up feeling like I never rested at all. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I haven't noticed moving any slower or anything. I mean, I sit at my little table in Mrs. Gable's room quite a bit. Though, she just ordered these new chairs for the kids—those wobbly stools, you know? They're supposed to help the kids who can't sit still. Toby has one, and he's always bouncing on it. It makes this squeaking noise on the linoleum that drives me a little crazy. The janitor, Mr. Davis, said he could put tennis balls on the bottom of them to help with the noise. People used to do that with walkers, remember? My grandmother had a walker with bright yellow tennis balls on the back legs. She used to walk down to the corner store every single morning to buy a newspaper, back when people still read the physical paper. Now everyone just looks at their phones for the news, which I think is why everyone is so stressed out all the time... \n\nBut, um, I'm sorry. No, I don't have any trouble sitting still myself. And I don't feel like I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. Just my normal pace, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do feel guilty a lot. It's like... well, for example, whenever anything goes wrong with the curriculum project I'm helping with at school, even something really small, I just spiral. I keep thinking I'm completely letting the students down. And then I start feeling like I'm just wasting all the sacrifices my parents made for me to have this kind of opportunity in the first place, you know, to get my degree and work in education. \n\nThey worked so incredibly hard. My dad was at the textile mill for thirty years. He used to bring home these wooden spools of thread that were almost empty, and my mom would use them for her sewing. She actually made all my clothes when I was little. I had this one dress, it was yellow with little white daisies on it, and I wore it for my fifth-grade picture. Remember how they used to do those school pictures with the weird laser backgrounds? Or the fake library bookshelf backgrounds. I always chose the bookshelf because I loved reading so much. I used to go to the public library every Saturday and check out the maximum number of books allowed. The librarian, Mrs. Gable—no relation to the teacher I work with now, just a coincidence—she would always save the new mystery novels for me... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the guilt. It just feels like I'm failing the kids and failing my parents if the curriculum project isn't absolutely perfect.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I... well, I don't really want to actively hurt myself. But sometimes, when I'm up late at night in my little studio room at home—that's where I do all the cutting and laminating for Mrs. Gable's class—I do have these thoughts. We actually turned the spare bedroom into that studio space after my son went off to college. He's at State now, studying civil engineering. I still can't believe how fast the time went. I was just looking at his old science fair projects the other day. He made this papier-mâché volcano in the third grade, and we used way too much baking soda and red food coloring. It erupted everywhere and completely ruined the kitchen rug. It was this hideous green shag rug anyway, so my husband, Mark, wasn't too upset about it. He actually spent that entire weekend tearing it up and putting down that snap-together laminate flooring. It looks like real wood, but it's so much easier to clean, especially when you're tracking in dirt from the garden... \n\nOh, gosh, I'm sorry. The thoughts. Right. It's just... on those late nights in the studio, I sometimes catch myself thinking that it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. I haven't made a plan or anything, I promise. But just having those thoughts pop into my head... they really scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told Mark about it the other day, because I realized I just couldn't keep handling them alone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been building for a while now... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess... yes, it's definitely interfering. I just feel so slow, like moving through molasses, you know? And it's really affecting my work. I've actually missed several teaching sessions lately, and I've been completely avoiding the student workshops. I just can't keep up with the responsibilities the way I used to. The workshops used to be my favorite part. We'd do these hands-on activities, like the time we had the kids build bridges out of dry spaghetti and marshmallows. Have you ever tried that? It's incredibly sticky. I bought the jumbo marshmallows by mistake, and this one little boy, Tommy, he kept trying to eat them instead of building. Tommy is such a sweet kid, his mom is the president of the PTA. She organizes the annual bake sale, and she makes these incredible lemon bars. I tried to get her recipe once, but she said it's a family secret. My grandmother had a secret recipe for peach cobbler. We used to go peach picking every August in Georgia when I was little. The fuzz always made me sneeze, but the peaches were so sweet and juicy, you had to eat them over the sink... 

But, right, the functioning. It's bad at home, too. I just withdraw from family plans, and my husband, Mark, has had to step in a lot more to handle things. I just don't feel safe trying to push through all of it alone anymore.

She actually made all my clothes when I was little. I had this one dress, it was yellow with little white daisies on it, and I wore it for my fifth-grade picture. Remember how they used to do those school pictures with the weird laser backgrounds? Or the fake library bookshelf backgrounds. I always chose the bookshelf because I loved reading so much. I used to go to the public library every Saturday and check out the maximum number of books allowed. The librarian, Mrs. Gable—no relation to the teacher I work with now, just a coincidence—she would always save the new mystery novels for me... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the guilt. It just feels like I'm failing the kids and failing my parents if the curriculum project isn't absolutely perfect.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I... well, I don't really want to actively hurt myself. But sometimes, when I'm up late at night in my little studio room at home—that's where I do all the cutting and laminating for Mrs. Gable's class—I do have these thoughts. We actually turned the spare bedroom into that studio space after my son went off to college. He's at State now, studying civil engineering. I still can't believe how fast the time went. I was just looking at his old science fair projects the other day. He made this papier-mâché volcano in the third grade, and we used way too much baking soda and red food coloring. It erupted everywhere and completely ruined the kitchen rug. It was this hideous green shag rug anyway, so my husband, Mark, wasn't too upset about it. He actually spent that entire weekend tearing it up and putting down that snap-together laminate flooring. It looks like real wood, but it's so much easier to clean, especially when you're tracking in dirt from the garden... \n\nOh, gosh, I'm sorry. The thoughts. Right. It's just... on those late nights in the studio, I sometimes catch myself thinking that it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. I haven't made a plan or anything, I promise. But just having those thoughts pop into my head... they really scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told Mark about it the other day, because I realized I just couldn't keep handling them alone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been building for a while now... We were going to have the kids build these little solar-powered kinetic sculptures. I remember when I was a kid, we made sculptures out of popsicle sticks and Elmer's glue. We didn't have solar panels back then, of course. My brother used to eat the glue, actually. He's a dentist now, which is pretty funny when you think about it. He lives out in Colorado with his wife, Sarah. They just bought this beautiful A-frame cabin near the mountains, and they've been sending pictures of the snow. I haven't seen real snow in years, not since we took that ski trip when my son was in middle school. He broke his wrist on the bunny hill the very first day, and we spent the rest of the vacation playing Scrabble in the lodge... \n\nBut, um, anyway. Yes, about eight months. I mean, I know I said that thing about the molasses a minute ago, but I'm not entirely sure if my actual physical movements are slower, if that makes sense. I haven't noticed any weight changes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been a complete mess, honestly. It's been all over the place for months now. I'll just lie there in bed at night, staring at the ceiling, and my brain just won't shut off. I keep replaying all these details from this grant application I was helping the school with... which reminds me, the principal, Mr. Henderson, he's been talking about using the grant money to renovate the old computer lab. They want to put in these new interactive smartboards, which I guess are supposed to be better for the kids' eyes than the old projectors. They're supposed to help the kids who can't sit still. Toby has one, and he's always bouncing on it. It makes this squeaking noise on the linoleum that drives me a little crazy. The janitor, Mr. Davis, said he could put tennis balls on the bottom of them to help with the noise. People used to do that with walkers, remember? My grandmother had a walker with bright yellow tennis balls on the back legs. She used to walk down to the corner store every single morning to buy a newspaper, back when people still read the physical paper. Now everyone just looks at their phones for the news, which I think is why everyone is so stressed out all the time... \n\nBut, um, I'm sorry. No, I don't have any trouble sitting still myself. I was just looking at his old science fair projects the other day. He made this papier-mâché volcano in the third grade, and we used way too much baking soda and red food coloring. It erupted everywhere and completely ruined the kitchen rug. It was this hideous green shag rug anyway, so my husband, Mark, wasn't too upset about it. He actually spent that entire weekend tearing it up and putting down that snap-together laminate flooring. It looks like real wood, but it's so much easier to clean, especially when you're tracking in dirt from the garden... \n\nOh, gosh, I'm sorry. The thoughts. Right. It's just... on those late nights in the studio, I sometimes catch myself thinking that it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. I just feel heavy on the inside, but I don't really know if my actual steps are slower than they used to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / teaching_assistant / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Weight? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any big changes with my clothes or anything. My weight has always been pretty steady, actually. Even after I had my son, I bounced back to my normal size fairly quickly. I remember my sister-in-law, Sarah—the one in Colorado—she was so jealous because she struggled for years to lose the baby weight after her twins were born. Twins! Can you imagine? They're identical boys, Leo and Lucas. They used to trick their teachers all the time in elementary school by swapping seats. It's funny how identical twins can have such different personalities, though. Leo is really outgoing and plays the trumpet in the marching band, but Lucas is much quieter and loves painting. He did this beautiful watercolor of a blue jay last spring that Sarah framed and put in their living room. I've always wished I could paint like that. I tried taking a watercolor class at the community center a few years ago, but my colors always just ended up looking like muddy water... 

But, um, weight loss. No, I haven't lost a significant amount of weight. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale, to be honest.

It's like... most days I feel like there's this heavy gray film over absolutely everything. I can be standing right there in a classroom full of students—I'm a teaching assistant, mostly with fourth graders this year. And they are usually just so full of energy. Just yesterday, one of the boys, Toby, brought in this massive caterpillar he found by the playground fence. It was bright green with these little yellow spots. The lead teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually wants to get a terrarium so the kids can watch it turn into a butterfly. Which I think is a lovely idea, really, though I don't know who is going to take care of it over the weekends. I guess she will take it home, or maybe the janitor... \n\nBut, um... sorry. What I mean is, I can be standing right in the middle of all that life in the classroom, or even just sitting at home on the couch with my husband, and I still just feel completely hollow inside. I'm just always right on the edge of tears, no matter what's going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think my weight has changed much. My clothes still fit the same. Though, speaking of clothes, I was trying to find my favorite blue cardigan the other day—the one with the pearl buttons. I bought it on clearance at that little boutique downtown, the one that used to be a bakery? They still have the original tin ceiling in there, it's really beautiful. The owner said she was thinking of painting it copper, but I told her she should leave it silver. It really opens up the space. \n\nBut anyway... um, no. I haven't noticed any weight changes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been a complete mess, honestly. It's been all over the place for months now. I'll just lie there in bed at night, staring at the ceiling, and my brain just won't shut off. I keep replaying all these details from this grant application I was helping the school with... which reminds me, the principal, Mr. Henderson, he's been talking about using the grant money to renovate the old computer lab. They want to put in these new interactive smartboards, which I guess are supposed to be better for the kids' eyes than the old projectors. I remember when we just had chalkboards, and you had to clap the erasers outside. The dust would get everywhere, all over your hands and clothes... \n\nOh, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yes, my sleep. I just lie awake thinking about the grant, and then I finally drift off, but I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. And then I'm just up. Even on the rare nights when I do manage to sleep a little longer, I still get up feeling like I never rested at all. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I haven't noticed moving any slower or anything. I mean, I sit at my little table in Mrs. Gable's room quite a bit. Though, she just ordered these new chairs for the kids—those wobbly stools, you know? They're supposed to help the kids who can't sit still. Toby has one, and he's always bouncing on it. It makes this squeaking noise on the linoleum that drives me a little crazy. The janitor, Mr. Davis, said he could put tennis balls on the bottom of them to help with the noise. People used to do that with walkers, remember? My grandmother had a walker with bright yellow tennis balls on the back legs. She used to walk down to the corner store every single morning to buy a newspaper, back when people still read the physical paper. Now everyone just looks at their phones for the news, which I think is why everyone is so stressed out all the time... \n\nBut, um, I'm sorry. No, I don't have any trouble sitting still myself. And I don't feel like I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. Just my normal pace, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do feel guilty a lot. It's like... well, for example, whenever anything goes wrong with the curriculum project I'm helping with at school, even something really small, I just spiral. I keep thinking I'm completely letting the students down. And then I start feeling like I'm just wasting all the sacrifices my parents made for me to have this kind of opportunity in the first place, you know, to get my degree and work in education. \n\nThey worked so incredibly hard. My dad was at the textile mill for thirty years. He used to bring home these wooden spools of thread that were almost empty, and my mom would use them for her sewing. She actually made all my clothes when I was little. I had this one dress, it was yellow with little white daisies on it, and I wore it for my fifth-grade picture. Remember how they used to do those school pictures with the weird laser backgrounds? Or the fake library bookshelf backgrounds. I always chose the bookshelf because I loved reading so much. I used to go to the public library every Saturday and check out the maximum number of books allowed. The librarian, Mrs. Gable—no relation to the teacher I work with now, just a coincidence—she would always save the new mystery novels for me... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the guilt. It just feels like I'm failing the kids and failing my parents if the curriculum project isn't absolutely perfect.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I... well, I don't really want to actively hurt myself. But sometimes, when I'm up late at night in my little studio room at home—that's where I do all the cutting and laminating for Mrs. Gable's class—I do have these thoughts. We actually turned the spare bedroom into that studio space after my son went off to college. He's at State now, studying civil engineering. I still can't believe how fast the time went. I was just looking at his old science fair projects the other day. He made this papier-mâché volcano in the third grade, and we used way too much baking soda and red food coloring. It erupted everywhere and completely ruined the kitchen rug. It was this hideous green shag rug anyway, so my husband, Mark, wasn't too upset about it. He actually spent that entire weekend tearing it up and putting down that snap-together laminate flooring. It looks like real wood, but it's so much easier to clean, especially when you're tracking in dirt from the garden... \n\nOh, gosh, I'm sorry. The thoughts. Right. It's just... on those late nights in the studio, I sometimes catch myself thinking that it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. I haven't made a plan or anything, I promise. But just having those thoughts pop into my head... they really scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told Mark about it the other day, because I realized I just couldn't keep handling them alone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been building for a while now... I'd say about eight months. It started right around the time I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. It sounded like such a wonderful idea at first, combining science and art. We were going to have the kids build these little solar-powered kinetic sculptures. I remember when I was a kid, we made sculptures out of popsicle sticks and Elmer's glue. We didn't have solar panels back then, of course. My brother used to eat the glue, actually. He's a dentist now, which is pretty funny when you think about it. He lives out in Colorado with his wife, Sarah. They just bought this beautiful A-frame cabin near the mountains, and they've been sending pictures of the snow. I haven't seen real snow in years, not since we took that ski trip when my son was in middle school. He broke his wrist on the bunny hill the very first day, and we spent the rest of the vacation playing Scrabble in the lodge... \n\nBut, um, anyway. Yes, about eight months. Ever since that curriculum project started, things have just been getting heavier and heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any psychomotor agitation or retardation that interferes with your social, occupational, or other areas of functioning?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yes, it's definitely interfering. I just feel so slow, like moving through molasses, you know? And it's really affecting my work. I've actually missed several teaching sessions lately, and I've been completely avoiding the student workshops. I just can't keep up with the responsibilities the way I used to. The workshops used to be my favorite part. We'd do these hands-on activities, like the time we had the kids build bridges out of dry spaghetti and marshmallows. Have you ever tried that? It's incredibly sticky. I bought the jumbo marshmallows by mistake, and this one little boy, Tommy, he kept trying to eat them instead of building. Tommy is such a sweet kid, his mom is the president of the PTA. She organizes the annual bake sale, and she makes these incredible lemon bars. I tried to get her recipe once, but she said it's a family secret. My grandmother had a secret recipe for peach cobbler. We used to go peach picking every August in Georgia when I was little. The fuzz always made me sneeze, but the peaches were so sweet and juicy, you had to eat them over the sink... \n\nBut, right, the functioning. It's bad at home, too. I just withdraw from family plans, and my husband, Mark, has had to step in a lot more to handle things. I just don't feel safe trying to push through all of it alone anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know I said that thing about the molasses a minute ago, but I'm not entirely sure if my actual physical movements are slower, if that makes sense. I haven't really noticed anyone waiting on me to walk or anything like that. It's hard to tell if my body is actually slow or if time is just passing strangely. Time is funny like that, isn't it? Especially with daylight savings recently. The clocks changing always completely throws off my morning routine. We have this beautiful antique grandfather clock in the front hallway that Mark inherited from his great-uncle Arthur, and it has to be wound by hand. Arthur was an amateur clockmaker down in Florida. He lived in this little stucco house in Boca Raton with these huge lime trees in the backyard. We visited him once when my son was just a toddler, and Arthur made us fresh key lime pie from scratch. It was so incredibly tart, it made my jaw ache just eating it, but it was delicious. He had this little terrier mix named Buster who kept trying to eat the fallen limes in the yard, and he would make the funniest face every time he bit into one... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I completely lost my train of thought. Moving slower? I really couldn't say for sure. I just feel heavy on the inside, but I don't really know if my actual steps are slower than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight? I... didn't we just talk about this? Or maybe I'm confusing it with the molasses thing. My memory has been so foggy lately. It's like that time we drove to Maine for a long weekend and hit that massive bank of fog on the highway. We couldn't see ten feet in front of the car. Mark was driving, and he was gripping the steering wheel so tight his knuckles were completely white. We ended up having to pull over at this tiny diner in the middle of nowhere just to wait it out. They had the best blueberry pancakes I've ever eaten, though. The blueberries were tiny and wild, not like those giant watery ones you get at the supermarket. The waitress was this older woman named Betty who had the most incredible collection of enamel pins on her apron. She told me she collected one from every state she'd ever visited. She was only missing North Dakota and Hawaii. I've always wanted to go to Hawaii, but the flight is just so long. My friend Susan went to Maui for her honeymoon, and she said the jet lag ruined the first three days of the trip...

But anyway, my body moving slowly? I really don't think so. I mean, I feel exhausted and heavy, but I haven't noticed my actual physical movements being slower than normal. Nobody has mentioned it to me, either.

We actually turned the spare bedroom into that studio space after my son went off to college. He's at State now, studying civil engineering. I still can't believe how fast the time went. I was just looking at his old science fair projects the other day. He made this papier-mâché volcano in the third grade, and we used way too much baking soda and red food coloring. It erupted everywhere and completely ruined the kitchen rug. It was this hideous green shag rug anyway, so my husband, Mark, wasn't too upset about it. He actually spent that entire weekend tearing it up and putting down that snap-together laminate flooring. We didn't have solar panels back then, of course. My brother used to eat the glue, actually. He's a dentist now, which is pretty funny when you think about it. He lives out in Colorado with his wife, Sarah. They just bought this beautiful A-frame cabin near the mountains, and they've been sending pictures of the snow. Even after I had my son, I bounced back to my normal size fairly quickly. I remember my sister-in-law, Sarah—the one in Colorado—she was so jealous because she struggled for years to lose the baby weight after her twins were born. Twins! Can you imagine? It's funny how identical twins can have such different personalities, though. Leo is really outgoing and plays the trumpet in the marching band, but Lucas is much quieter and loves painting. He did this beautiful watercolor of a blue jay last spring that Sarah framed and put in their living room. I've always wished I could paint like that. I tried taking a watercolor class at the community center a few years ago, but my colors always just ended up looking like muddy water... \n\nBut, um, weight loss. No, I haven't lost a significant amount of weight. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I... didn't we just talk about this? It's funny how identical twins can have such different personalities, though. Leo is really outgoing and plays the trumpet in the marching band, but Lucas is much quieter and loves painting. He did this beautiful watercolor of a blue jay last spring that Sarah framed and put in their living room. I've always wished I could paint like that. I tried taking a watercolor class at the community center a few years ago, but my colors always just ended up looking like muddy water... \n\nBut, um, weight loss. No, I haven't lost a significant amount of weight. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I... didn't we just talk about this? Or maybe I'm confusing it with the molasses thing. My memory has been so foggy lately. It's like that time we drove to Maine for a long weekend and hit that massive bank of fog on the highway. We couldn't see ten feet in front of the car. Mark was driving, and he was gripping the steering wheel so tight his knuckles were completely white. We ended up having to pull over at this tiny diner in the middle of nowhere just to wait it out. They had the best blueberry pancakes I've ever eaten, though. The blueberries were tiny and wild, not like those giant watery ones you get at the supermarket. The waitress was this older woman named Betty who had the most incredible collection of enamel pins on her apron. She told me she collected one from every state she'd ever visited. She was only missing North Dakota and Hawaii. I've always wanted to go to Hawaii, but the flight is just so long. My friend Susan went to Maui for her honeymoon, and she said the jet lag ruined the first three days of the trip...\n\nBut anyway, my body moving slowly? I really don't think so. I mean, I feel exhausted and heavy, but I haven't noticed my actual physical movements being slower than normal. Nobody has mentioned it to me, either.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your feelings of sadness and loss of interest are interfering with your daily life a lot, or just a little?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / teaching_assistant / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I've missed several teaching sessions and have been avoiding student workshops because I can't keep up with the responsibilities the way I used to. At home, my husband has had to step in more because I withdraw from family plans and don't feel safe pushing through alone.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, a lot, I think. It's definitely a lot. I've actually missed several of my teaching sessions lately, and I've been completely avoiding the student workshops. I just can't keep up with the responsibilities the way I used to. The last workshop I was supposed to help with was for the seventh graders, and they were doing this massive diorama project on the solar system. Which is always such a mess, really, because of those styrofoam balls. You paint them and then the paint never dries right, and it flakes off everywhere. My son did a project like that when he was that age, and we ended up with silver glitter on the dining room rug for literally three years. We tried everything to get it out—vacuuming, steam cleaning, even this special foam spray my neighbor recommended. She swore by it because she had three golden retrievers, and you know how much they shed. Beautiful dogs, though. She had one named Bailey who would actually carry the morning paper up the driveway in his mouth, just like in the movies. He was so gentle, he never even tore the plastic sleeve...

Wait, I'm sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, interfering with my life. Yes, it's... it's a lot. Even at home, Mark has had to step in so much more. I just kind of withdraw from all our family plans lately. I don't feel safe trying to push through it alone anymore, so I just stay back. So, yes, I'd say it's interfering quite a bit.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally brought me in is that I just... I don't feel like myself anymore. I used to make time for so many things, like painting, hiking, singing in the choir, and our big Sunday family gatherings. Which, actually, my sister has been hosting lately because she finally finished her kitchen remodel. It took them almost eight months, can you believe that? The contractors just kept delaying and delaying because of some supply chain issue with the cabinets, or maybe it was the tile. She did get this gorgeous farmhouse sink out of it, though. I told her she should put some little potted succulents by the window above it, since it gets that good morning light... \n\nBut, um, anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. It's like... most days I feel like there's this heavy gray film over absolutely everything. I can be standing right there in a classroom full of students—I'm a teaching assistant, mostly with fourth graders this year. And they are usually just so full of energy. Just yesterday, one of the boys, Toby, brought in this massive caterpillar he found by the playground fence. It was bright green with these little yellow spots. The lead teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually wants to get a terrarium so the kids can watch it turn into a butterfly. Which I think is a lovely idea, really, though I don't know who is going to take care of it over the weekends. I guess she will take it home, or maybe the janitor... \n\nBut, um... sorry. What I mean is, I can be standing right in the middle of all that life in the classroom, or even just sitting at home on the couch with my husband, and I still just feel completely hollow inside. I'm just always right on the edge of tears, no matter what's going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think my weight has changed much. My clothes still fit the same. Though, speaking of clothes, I was trying to find my favorite blue cardigan the other day—the one with the pearl buttons. I bought it on clearance at that little boutique downtown, the one that used to be a bakery? They still have the original tin ceiling in there, it's really beautiful. The owner said she was thinking of painting it copper, but I told her she should leave it silver. It really opens up the space. \n\nBut anyway... um, no. I haven't noticed any weight changes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been a complete mess, honestly. It's been all over the place for months now. I'll just lie there in bed at night, staring at the ceiling, and my brain just won't shut off. I keep replaying all these details from this grant application I was helping the school with... which reminds me, the principal, Mr. Henderson, he's been talking about using the grant money to renovate the old computer lab. They want to put in these new interactive smartboards, which I guess are supposed to be better for the kids' eyes than the old projectors. I remember when we just had chalkboards, and you had to clap the erasers outside. The dust would get everywhere, all over your hands and clothes... \n\nOh, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yes, my sleep. I just lie awake thinking about the grant, and then I finally drift off, but I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. And then I'm just up. Even on the rare nights when I do manage to sleep a little longer, I still get up feeling like I never rested at all. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I haven't noticed moving any slower or anything. I mean, I sit at my little table in Mrs. Gable's room quite a bit. Though, she just ordered these new chairs for the kids—those wobbly stools, you know? They're supposed to help the kids who can't sit still. Toby has one, and he's always bouncing on it. It makes this squeaking noise on the linoleum that drives me a little crazy. The janitor, Mr. Davis, said he could put tennis balls on the bottom of them to help with the noise. People used to do that with walkers, remember? My grandmother had a walker with bright yellow tennis balls on the back legs. She used to walk down to the corner store every single morning to buy a newspaper, back when people still read the physical paper. Now everyone just looks at their phones for the news, which I think is why everyone is so stressed out all the time... \n\nBut, um, I'm sorry. No, I don't have any trouble sitting still myself. And I don't feel like I'm moving in slow motion or anything like that. Just my normal pace, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do feel guilty a lot. It's like... well, for example, whenever anything goes wrong with the curriculum project I'm helping with at school, even something really small, I just spiral. I keep thinking I'm completely letting the students down. And then I start feeling like I'm just wasting all the sacrifices my parents made for me to have this kind of opportunity in the first place, you know, to get my degree and work in education. \n\nThey worked so incredibly hard. My dad was at the textile mill for thirty years. He used to bring home these wooden spools of thread that were almost empty, and my mom would use them for her sewing. She actually made all my clothes when I was little. I had this one dress, it was yellow with little white daisies on it, and I wore it for my fifth-grade picture. Remember how they used to do those school pictures with the weird laser backgrounds? Or the fake library bookshelf backgrounds. I always chose the bookshelf because I loved reading so much. I used to go to the public library every Saturday and check out the maximum number of books allowed. The librarian, Mrs. Gable—no relation to the teacher I work with now, just a coincidence—she would always save the new mystery novels for me... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the guilt. It just feels like I'm failing the kids and failing my parents if the curriculum project isn't absolutely perfect.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I... well, I don't really want to actively hurt myself. But sometimes, when I'm up late at night in my little studio room at home—that's where I do all the cutting and laminating for Mrs. Gable's class—I do have these thoughts. We actually turned the spare bedroom into that studio space after my son went off to college. He's at State now, studying civil engineering. I still can't believe how fast the time went. I was just looking at his old science fair projects the other day. He made this papier-mâché volcano in the third grade, and we used way too much baking soda and red food coloring. It erupted everywhere and completely ruined the kitchen rug. It was this hideous green shag rug anyway, so my husband, Mark, wasn't too upset about it. He actually spent that entire weekend tearing it up and putting down that snap-together laminate flooring. It looks like real wood, but it's so much easier to clean, especially when you're tracking in dirt from the garden... \n\nOh, gosh, I'm sorry. The thoughts. Right. It's just... on those late nights in the studio, I sometimes catch myself thinking that it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. I haven't made a plan or anything, I promise. But just having those thoughts pop into my head... they really scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told Mark about it the other day, because I realized I just couldn't keep handling them alone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been building for a while now... I'd say about eight months. It started right around the time I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. It sounded like such a wonderful idea at first, combining science and art. We were going to have the kids build these little solar-powered kinetic sculptures. I remember when I was a kid, we made sculptures out of popsicle sticks and Elmer's glue. We didn't have solar panels back then, of course. My brother used to eat the glue, actually. He's a dentist now, which is pretty funny when you think about it. He lives out in Colorado with his wife, Sarah. They just bought this beautiful A-frame cabin near the mountains, and they've been sending pictures of the snow. I haven't seen real snow in years, not since we took that ski trip when my son was in middle school. He broke his wrist on the bunny hill the very first day, and we spent the rest of the vacation playing Scrabble in the lodge... \n\nBut, um, anyway. Yes, about eight months. Ever since that curriculum project started, things have just been getting heavier and heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any psychomotor agitation or retardation that interferes with your social, occupational, or other areas of functioning?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... yes, it's definitely interfering. I just feel so slow, like moving through molasses, you know? And it's really affecting my work. I've actually missed several teaching sessions lately, and I've been completely avoiding the student workshops. I just can't keep up with the responsibilities the way I used to. The workshops used to be my favorite part. We'd do these hands-on activities, like the time we had the kids build bridges out of dry spaghetti and marshmallows. Have you ever tried that? It's incredibly sticky. I bought the jumbo marshmallows by mistake, and this one little boy, Tommy, he kept trying to eat them instead of building. Tommy is such a sweet kid, his mom is the president of the PTA. She organizes the annual bake sale, and she makes these incredible lemon bars. I tried to get her recipe once, but she said it's a family secret. My grandmother had a secret recipe for peach cobbler. We used to go peach picking every August in Georgia when I was little. The fuzz always made me sneeze, but the peaches were so sweet and juicy, you had to eat them over the sink... \n\nBut, right, the functioning. It's bad at home, too. I just withdraw from family plans, and my husband, Mark, has had to step in a lot more to handle things. I just don't feel safe trying to push through all of it alone anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know I said that thing about the molasses a minute ago, but I'm not entirely sure if my actual physical movements are slower, if that makes sense. I haven't really noticed anyone waiting on me to walk or anything like that. It's hard to tell if my body is actually slow or if time is just passing strangely. Time is funny like that, isn't it? Especially with daylight savings recently. The clocks changing always completely throws off my morning routine. We have this beautiful antique grandfather clock in the front hallway that Mark inherited from his great-uncle Arthur, and it has to be wound by hand. Arthur was an amateur clockmaker down in Florida. He lived in this little stucco house in Boca Raton with these huge lime trees in the backyard. We visited him once when my son was just a toddler, and Arthur made us fresh key lime pie from scratch. It was so incredibly tart, it made my jaw ache just eating it, but it was delicious. He had this little terrier mix named Buster who kept trying to eat the fallen limes in the yard, and he would make the funniest face every time he bit into one... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I completely lost my train of thought. Moving slower? I really couldn't say for sure. I just feel heavy on the inside, but I don't really know if my actual steps are slower than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any big changes with my clothes or anything. My weight has always been pretty steady, actually. Even after I had my son, I bounced back to my normal size fairly quickly. I remember my sister-in-law, Sarah—the one in Colorado—she was so jealous because she struggled for years to lose the baby weight after her twins were born. Twins! Can you imagine? They're identical boys, Leo and Lucas. They used to trick their teachers all the time in elementary school by swapping seats. It's funny how identical twins can have such different personalities, though. Leo is really outgoing and plays the trumpet in the marching band, but Lucas is much quieter and loves painting. He did this beautiful watercolor of a blue jay last spring that Sarah framed and put in their living room. I've always wished I could paint like that. Or maybe I'm confusing it with the molasses thing. My memory has been so foggy lately. It's like that time we drove to Maine for a long weekend and hit that massive bank of fog on the highway. We couldn't see ten feet in front of the car. Mark was driving, and he was gripping the steering wheel so tight his knuckles were completely white. We ended up having to pull over at this tiny diner in the middle of nowhere just to wait it out. They had the best blueberry pancakes I've ever eaten, though. The blueberries were tiny and wild, not like those giant watery ones you get at the supermarket. The waitress was this older woman named Betty who had the most incredible collection of enamel pins on her apron. She told me she collected one from every state she'd ever visited.
